- **Current use:** a registered session resumes in the mode its transcript records (`transcriptLaunch`, architecture.md § Code Mode sessions, Registered sessions).

## M120. Focus view's row of running tool calls on the screen, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Claude Code CLI 2.1.330 (native build), Opus 5.5, on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; node-pty 1.1.0 and @xterm/headless at 120 × 40.
- **Experiment:** a Code Mode session started with `codeModeSettings()` (Focus view, classic renderer) was asked to write a sentence before each of two shell commands; its screen was read every 300 ms. Then the screen copy of the operator's session 311b1ad4 that the web page showed with `· summary` and `Working… (ctrl+o to expand)` was compared with its transcript and the hub's log.
- **Result:**
  - While a tool call runs, Focus view draws a row `● Working… (ctrl+o to expand)` under the text written before it, after a blank line, with its `●` in grey (`#999999`) blinking: drawn blank, the row reads `  Working… (ctrl+o to expand)`, a reply's continuation line, so the screen reader added it to the reply's copy as a last paragraph (recorded in `packages/conversation/test/fixtures/screens/focus-working.txt`).
  - In 311b1ad4 the text before a tool call was saved only as a thinking block (`thinkingDisplay: "updates"`), drawn as `● <text> · summary`; with the row added, the `· summary` was not at the copy's end and stayed, so the copy's key never matched the transcript copy's. The transcript copy was published as a reply of its own, and the hub restarted 20 s later while the screen copy still waited, which left it published as a reply; no withdrawal was logged. The message holding that text, shown as the operator's, was one the operator sent from the page (its transcript line came 4 s after "Screen reading bug:"), not a screen copy taken as a message.
- **Current use:** the screen reader ends a reply's copy at such a row and skips the row itself, and a restarted hub takes up the screen copies left waiting (architecture.md § Reading a session).

## M121. Claude Code's input box without TERM, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Claude Code CLI 2.1.330 (native build), Haiku 5.5, classic renderer (`tui: "default"`), Code Mode on, on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; node-pty 1.1.0 (ConPTY), @xterm/headless 6.0.0.
- **Symptom:** every message typed into a held session since the typing timing lines began logged `paste→echo not seen (no input box on the screen)` in hub.log, for Switchboard-started and registered sessions alike.
- **Experiment:** `.wip/box-exp/exp.mjs` in the send-freeze worktree (not committed) spawned `claude --model haiku --settings {"viewMode":"focus","tui":"default"}` with `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_CODE_MODE=1` in node-pty at 120×40 on a scratch `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` holding a copy of the operator's sign-in (deleted after), wrote its output into a headless terminal, and read the serialized screen with `readInputScreen` idle, after a paste, during and after a one-word turn and after Ctrl+U, with `TERM=xterm-256color`, without `TERM`, with `TERM=dumb`, `NO_COLOR=1` and `FORCE_COLOR=0`.
- **Result:**
  - With `TERM=xterm-256color`, with or without `COLORTERM`, the input box's prompt line is `❯ `, and the box and a pasted draft were read in every state.
  - Without `TERM`, as Windows gives a process VS Code starts, and with `TERM=dumb`, the prompt line is `> `, a menu's cursor is `>`, and `⚠` is `‼`; the rules and the `●` reply bullet are unchanged. The box was never found, so a paste was never seen and a draft never read.
  - With `NO_COLOR=1` the placeholder is not dim, so it reads as a draft.
- **Not verified:** the environment of the running hub and holder, which were left alone; Linux.
- **Current use:** the Code Mode environment sets `TERM`, and the input box and menus read a `>` prompt line under a rule and a `>` menu cursor (architecture.md § Code Mode sessions, § Typing a message).

## M122. Where Mic on's start spends its time, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** sherpa-onnx-node 2.0.1, its CUDA 12 build, the fp32 and int8 Nemotron 0.6B 160 ms streaming models, on an NVIDIA RTX 2000 Ada (16 GB), Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300, Windows PowerShell 5.1; copies of the models outside the Switchboard home; the operator's voice and hub logs, read only.
- **Evidence:** the voice log of a phone tap that took the voice and turned the mic on: `voice device: the phone` at 12:35:14.864, the first `listening:` line at 12:35:23.130, 8.3 s later; the hub log's voice socket `hello` came within 1 ms of the move. The voice process had started at 12:27:17 and had not had the mic on since, so its recogniser was not loaded. A later tap, at 12:36:00.264, listened within 2 s.
- **Method:** a recogniser created and decoded in a fresh Node.js process on each device; then the voice process's `Dictation` driven from Mic on to listening with the real recogniser and stand-ins for capture and the other-app watcher: the phone's, which answer at once, and this computer's, the real `micuse.ps1` watcher and PowerShell compiling `commcapture.ps1`'s types without opening a device. No microphone was opened and nothing was played.
- **Result:**
  - Creating the GPU recogniser took 5.1 s in the first run after the models were copied and 2.6 s with them in the file cache; another for other terms 3.3 s. Its first decode of a second of audio took 0.45 to 0.9 s, later ones 0.18 s. The CPU int8 recogniser took 1.7 to 2.6 s, with no slow first decode.
  - The other-app watcher's first answer came 0.3 to 0.5 s after it started; PowerShell starting and compiling the capture helper's types took 0.7 to 0.8 s, 0.15 s of it the compile.
  - Mic on to listening in the voice process, recogniser not loaded: 2.7 to 4.5 s for the phone; for this computer 3.0 to 3.4 s, with the watcher started while the recogniser loaded, where the two ran one after the other before. Recogniser loaded ahead: 13 ms for the phone, 0.9 s for this computer (other-app check 0.4 s, capture helper 0.5 s).
- **Not verified:** the page's own steps on an iPhone (the microphone permission prompt, the audio worklet's load over the remote host), which the page now reports in the hub log; whether iOS asks for the microphone again within a page's life after the page released it.
- **Current use:** the recogniser loads ahead of Mic on, and each start is timed in the voice and hub logs (architecture.md § Dictation).

## M124. Background agents and shell commands after a turn ends, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Claude Code CLI 2.1.330 (native build), Haiku 5.5, on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300.
- **Inspection:** the hook input schemas and the `background_tasks` builder in the 2.1.330 binary.
- **Experiment:** in a scratch git repository, with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` on a scratch folder holding a copy of the operator's sign-in files, deleted afterwards, and a hook that logged every payload of `SessionStart`, `UserPromptSubmit`, `PostToolBatch`, `Notification`, `SubagentStart`, `SubagentStop`, `TaskCreated`, `TaskCompleted`, `Stop` and `SessionEnd`, `claude -p` with `--permission-mode bypassPermissions`, outside Code Mode, was asked in one reply to start an `Agent` call with `run_in_background: true` and a `Bash` `sleep 25` with `run_in_background: true`, then end its turn. The subagent itself started `sleep 40` in the background.
- **Result:**
  - `Stop` and `SubagentStop` carry `background_tasks`, the session's background work that is running or pending: one entry per task with `id`, `type`, `status` and `description`, plus `command` for a `shell` and `agent_type` for a `subagent`. The binary maps task kinds to the types `subagent` (an `Agent` call), `shell` (a Bash or PowerShell command), `code` (a Code Mode script, M80), `monitor`, `workflow`, `MCP task`, `teammate`, `dream`, `auto-mode scan` and `cloud session`; foreground work and ended tasks are left out.
  - The first `Stop`, 6 s in, listed the subagent and the shell. A shell a subagent starts belongs to the session: the subagent's `SubagentStop` listed its own `sleep 40` beside the others.
  - Each task that ends wakes the session: a `UserPromptSubmit` whose `prompt` is a `<task-notification>` naming the task, a turn, and a `Stop` whose list no longer has it. Here at 7.9 s for the subagent (Stop at 11.1 s with the two shells) and at 30.6 s for `sleep 25` (Stop at 31.5 s with only `sleep 40`). The print-mode run then exited at 38 s with `sleep 40` still listed.
  - `SubagentStart` and `SubagentStop` fired for the background agent, both with its `agent_id`; `TaskCreated` and `TaskCompleted` did not fire, since they are for the task list, not background tasks.
- **Not verified:** a background or Code Mode session (only print mode was run); a task that ends while the session waits on the operator; whether a session whose last task ends without a wake (as when the task is stopped) gets a `Stop`; Linux.
- **Current use:** the hook writer keeps each listed task's type, and a turn that ended with background tasks shows "N agents running" at the end of the conversation (architecture.md § Claude Code runner, § Conversation view).

## M125. Which scroller a touch pan scrolls in Chrome when inner boxes contain overscroll, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Chrome 158.0.8127.36 headless on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; Switchboard at `2b467896` with the touch-scroll fix applied.
- **Experiment:** a scratch page with the built `page.css`, `theme.css` and `codicon.css` and the page's own markup: a conversation pane (`.scroll`) holding plain reply text, a wide and a narrow `.body` table, a wide code block, inline artifact rows with a long and a short title, an answer reference above a message, a question card and a prompt screen; a header menu (`.menu`) and a composer menu (`.composer-menu`) of 30 to 40 rows with cut titles; an artifact pane (`.scroll.framed` > `.artifact-view` > `.artifact-doc`) with text, a wide table and code; a Status pane; and a long Artifacts list. Each target was centred in its pane, then `Input.dispatchTouchEvent` with touch emulation on (1,280 px desktop, and 393 px with `mobile: true`) dragged a finger 180 px up from it over 12 moves, and the pane's `scrollTop` was read; a horizontal drag on the wide table read its `scrollLeft`.
- **Result:**
  - With `* { overscroll-behavior: contain; }`, a vertical drag that started on plain text or the question card's options scrolled its pane by about 210 px, but one that started on any box with `overflow` other than `visible` scrolled nothing: tables and code blocks with `overflow-x: auto`, whether or not they were wider than their box, and boxes that clip text with `overflow: hidden` (an artifact's title, an answer reference's question and answer, a menu row's title, which kept a long menu from scrolling), whether or not the text was cut. Chrome gave the pan to that innermost scroll container, which could not scroll vertically and, with `contain`, passed nothing on. The same at both widths.
  - With `overscroll-behavior: contain` only on the panes, popups and dialogs that scroll, and `auto` on everything inside them, every drag scrolled its pane by 165 to 212 px (the spread is the fling after release), and the horizontal drag still scrolled the wide table by about 208 px.
- **Not verified:** a real touch screen (the operator's Surface), Edge, WebKit on an iPhone; an HTML artifact's sandboxed frame, whose document scrolls itself.
- **Current use:** the web page's scrollers (`page.css`, architecture.md § Web page); `check-shell.mjs` accepts a scroller without `contain` only inside one that has it, and drags the conversation from a table in a reply.

## M126. A light console for Claude Code's dark theme, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Claude Code 2.1.330 (`claude.exe`), @xterm/xterm 6.0.0 with its DOM renderer, VS Code 1.147.0 (`workbench.desktop.main.js`, `theme-defaults`), headless Chromium through `playwright-cli` with the colour scheme emulated.
- **Claude Code's themes, by source inspection:** the binary holds four 72-key theme objects (`dark`, `light` and their daltonized variants) of `rgb(r,g,b)` strings under the same keys, so each dark colour has a light counterpart for the same purpose, kept apart for text and for backgrounds, because one value such as 255,255,255 can be text in one use and a background in another: a sent message's row 55,55,55 becomes 240,240,240, its text 255,255,255 becomes 0,0,0, secondary text 153,153,153 becomes 102,102,102, the mode line's amber 255,193,7 becomes 150,108,30, a diff's added row 34,92,43 becomes 105,219,124. Two collisions: 177,185,249 is `permission`, `suggestion` and `rate_limit_fill` (light 87,105,247) and `remember` (light 0,0,255); 0,0,0 is `inverseText` (light 255,255,255) and `clawd_background` (black in both).
- **VS Code's terminal:** light defaults `#000000 #cd3131 #107C10 #949800 #0451a5 #bc05bc #0598bc #555555`, bright `#666666 #f14c4c #14CE14 #b5ba00 #3b8eea #d670d6 #29b8db #a5a5a5`; Light Modern sets `terminal.foreground` #3B3B3B and `terminalCursor.foreground` #005FB8 on the panel's #F8F8F8; `terminal.integrated.minimumContrastRatio` defaults to 4.5.
- **xterm.js's minimum contrast:** applied per cell to the text against its background, 24-bit colours included, except box-drawing and block glyphs. Dim text gets half the ratio; a named or default colour that already meets it is then drawn by a class rule at half opacity, so its final contrast falls below even that, while 24-bit dim text is drawn undimmed (DOM renderer, `DomRendererRowFactory.ts`, `DomRenderer.ts`).
- **Measured** on a recorded Claude Code 2.1.327 screen at 120 by 40 with added Claude Code dark theme rows (a diff, tool marks, message backgrounds) and every named colour as normal, bright, dim, bold and background: in the light scheme with the rewrite and a 4.5 minimum, every non-dim text span measured at least 4.5:1 and the prompt's box-drawing rule 3.25:1; dim text measured 1.65:1 (yellow) to 3.93:1 (black) with xterm.js's own dim rules, and 3.01:1 to 3.93:1 with the console's light dim colours (each named colour and the default raised to 4.5:1, then faded towards the background by up to half while it keeps 3:1). The dark scheme with no minimum, as before, measured 1.18:1 for black text and 1.1:1 to 4.4:1 for dim text; a 4.5 minimum there turned white text on Claude Code's 56,166,96 word highlight dark green, so the dark scheme keeps none and xterm.js's own dim colours.
- **Not verified:** Claude Code's `light` or `-ansi` themes in the console (the light rewrite assumes the dark theme), a Codex terminal's colours, a real phone, and the selection drawn over text.
- **Current use:** the web page's console colours ([architecture.md](architecture.md) § Web page, Console). Re-run after upgrading Claude Code or xterm.js.

## M127. Phone dictation cut off while a reply plays, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** the voice process and phone page on main at 2b467896; an iPhone running the installed page in Safari over Tailscale, with Bluetooth earbuds, the page's microphone opened with `echoCancellation: true` under the `play-and-record` audio session.
- **Evidence:** the operator's `voice.log`, `hub.log` and `voice/fragments-2026-11-22.jsonl`, read only, for the phone sessions of 2026-11-22 (02:22 to 18:58 UTC).
- **Result:**
  - Phone capture said it cancelled no echo, so under `voice.duplex` `auto` it began each Mic on half-duplex and stayed so until two echo checks in a row heard none. A check needs about 3 s of playback, so listening was paused through 10 to 20 s of every first reply after Mic on, and every Mic on started the check over.
  - At 18:55:05 the turn model ended "Alright, so I'm now on my iPhone with the mic turned on and earbuds" after 2.3 s of quiet. The reply began at 18:55:11 while the operator went on; the capture went deaf, cutting the utterance after "I've checked" and keeping it unsent, and dropped everything said for the next 15 s. Full duplex came at 18:55:26, and the operator's voice stopped the reply at 637 of 648 characters at 18:55:29; "I've checked" was then sent with "Doubt number", 27 s after it was said, the words between lost. 17:37, 17:39, 17:40, 18:31 and 18:32 show the same cut.
  - In about 60 echo checks on the phone that day, with earbuds, every one of a stretch where the operator was quiet heard none: level -60 to -83 dBFS, correlation at most 0.46. The one `echo` verdict (18:31:01, -21.8 dBFS, r 0.58) came 6 s after the operator had talked over a reply, so it may have been his voice; it made capture half-duplex again and cut him again.
  - Separately, one spoken thought became two messages when the turn model ended it at a short pause: "We're still having audio duplexing issues" was sent at 18:55:58 after 1.2 s of quiet (turn model p 0.16), and "Started speaking a second sentence" 5 s later as a message of its own. The same pattern (p 0.1 to 0.4, 0.7 to 1.6 s of earned quiet, the next utterance within 5 s) appears through the day.
- **Not verified:** how much echo iOS's voice processing leaves with the phone's loudspeaker rather than earbuds; whether a web page can tell the output route on iOS (Safari lists no audio outputs).
- **Current use:** phone capture counts as capture that cancels the echo and listens while the voice plays from the first moment (architecture.md § Remote access, Voice on a phone; § Dictation), and a reply waits to start while the operator speaks and for a moment after (architecture.md § Voice check and barge-in).

## M128. Sign-in cookies on an unpacked extension's requests, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Playwright's Chromium 155.0.8059.12 (`chromium-1247`), headless (`--headless=new`), a scratch profile, the extension loaded with `--load-extension` and `--disable-features=DisableLoadExtensionCommandLineSwitch`; Windows 11.
- **Method:** a test HTTPS server on `127.0.0.1:8443` for a name mapped to it with `--host-resolver-rules`, certificate errors ignored. A tab opened its `/set`, which answered `Set-Cookie: sb_page=abc; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict; Path=/`; a Manifest V3 service worker with host permission `https://<name>/*` (no port) then fetched the server, and the server logged each request's `Cookie`, `Origin` and `Sec-Fetch-Site`. The same day, the built extension, downloaded from a scratch hub through a stand-in for `tailscale serve` and loaded unpacked, read and posted to that hub (architecture.md § Browser extension).
- **Result:**
  - The service worker's `GET`s, with and without `credentials: 'include'`, and its JSON `POST` all carried the `SameSite=Strict`, `HttpOnly`, `Secure` cookie the tab had been given, with `Sec-Fetch-Site: none`. A host permission without a port matched the server on port 8443.
  - The `GET`s carried no `Origin`; the `POST` carried `Origin: chrome-extension://<ID>`.
  - Before the tab signed in, the extension's read through the scratch hub was refused with 401; after it, reads answered 200 and a snooze reached the hub, which answered 404 for a ticket it did not have.
- **Not verified:** branded Chrome and Edge, which ignore `--load-extension` from Chrome 137; Firefox, whose extension requests treat `SameSite` cookies differently; a real `tailscale serve`.
- **Current use:** a browser extension downloaded on a paired device reaches the hub through the web page's origin with the page's sign-in (architecture.md § Browser extension, PRD R121).

## M129. Where a paired device's Mic on start waits, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** the voice process, hub and page on main at af8da476; sherpa-onnx-node 2.0.1 with its CUDA 12 build and the fp32 Nemotron model on an NVIDIA RTX 2000 Ada; headless Chrome 158.0.8127.36 and Edge 158.0.4370.33 on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300.
- **Evidence:** the operator's `voice.log` and `hub.log` for 2026-11-22, read only, for Mic on taps from the Surface's Edge page ("Windows · Edge" voice socket) and the iPhone.
  - From the hub's `mic` command to listening took 2 to 25 ms in every start but two, both the first after the voice process restarted: 20:02:06 (6749 ms) and 21:03:16 (7079 ms). In the first, the process restarted at 19:59:52 with no device, the phone took the voice at 20:01:41 before any target was set, so nothing loaded then, and Mic on loaded the recogniser itself (`recogniser loaded 6743 ms`). In the second, the voice process had restarted onto a new build at 20:56:18 and stayed up through two later hub restarts; the target came at 20:58:51 while no device had the voice, so nothing loaded ahead (the load ahead waited for a device); the phone took the voice at 21:03:08.708, and `recogniser loaded ahead of the mic in 6631 ms, its first decode 620 ms` at 21:03:16.024, the GPU check's verdict logged 1 s into the load. The page's own part of that start, from its voice socket opening to its `mic` command, was about 0.25 s.
  - The hub log has no `voice: mic on tap` line at all: the hub never passed the page's `mic-timing` to its voice control, so the page's steps M122 relied on were never logged.
- **Method:** a scratch harness, not committed, bundled `phoneVoice.ts` from source into a page served by a test-owned loopback server with a stand-in voice socket that welcomes the `hello`, and ran it in headless Chrome and Edge with `--use-fake-device-for-media-stream`, `--use-fake-ui-for-media-stream`, `--autoplay-policy=no-user-gesture-required` and `--mute-audio` on scratch profiles: each browser primitive alone, then `PhoneVoice.micOn` cold, again with the voice still on the page, and cold again in the same page; with no delay, and with 60 ms before the welcome and 120 ms before the worklet's answer as a remote host might add. Then the same with the worklet served `immutable` and fetched once at page load. Nothing played aloud.
- **Result:**
  - Creating the first audio context took 30 to 75 ms, resuming it under a millisecond, the worklet's `addModule` 4 to 18 ms locally, `getUserMedia` with the fake device 19 to 52 ms, the socket's open and welcome 3 to 20 ms. The page already starts the audio, socket and microphone in the tap's own call and waits for them together: `micOn` cold took 84 to 88 ms, 4 to 10 ms with the voice still on the page, and 17 to 25 ms cold again.
  - With the delays, the worklet was fetched again by every cold start, because the hub served it `no-cache`: 157 to 183 ms cold. Served `immutable` and fetched once at page load, `addModule` took 3 to 4 ms with one fetch in all, and `micOn` cold 79 to 109 ms, the rest the socket's welcome.
- **Not verified:** the page's steps on the iPhone and the Surface themselves, including the microphone prompt and WebKit's cache for a worklet; a load ahead that blocks the voice process while a phone takes the voice in the seconds after a restart.
- **Current use:** the hub logs a page's `mic-timing`; the recogniser loads ahead once the voice process has its settings and for each target, whether or not a device has the voice; the audio worklet is named by its content's hash, kept by the browser and fetched when voice is on (architecture.md § Dictation).

## M130. How Claude Code draws a Markdown link in a Code Mode session's terminal, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Claude Code 2.1.330, classic renderer, in the terminal holder's headless terminal; the hub store of 2026-11-22.
- **Method:** a screen copy of session 311b1ad4 showed beside its transcript copy until the hub withdrew it ("no transcript copy came", 21:12:59Z). Its stored text was compared with its transcript copy, then all 69 screen copies in the store, every one withdrawn, with the transcript copies within a minute of each; the link case of the markdown renderer in the Claude Code binary was read.
- **Result:**
  - The screen draws `[text](target)` as `text (target)`, for `file:///`, `https:` and relative targets alike: "changes.ts:280 (file:///D:/git/switchboard/packages/hub/src/changes.ts#L280)". A link whose text is its target is drawn as the target alone, a `mailto:` link without the scheme, and a title as ` ("title")` after it. A long target wraps mid-word like any other text.
  - The letters of the target made the screen copy's key differ from the transcript copy's, which counted the link's text only, so neither copy matched the other: 32 of the 69 withdrawn screen copies, from 2026-11-19 to 2026-11-22, had a link with its target drawn after it. A screen copy of a long reply read after its transcript copy was mostly kept back as a redraw of it, since their first 80 letters agree, but one with a link in those letters, or read before its transcript copy, was published and shown twice.
  - A bare `www.` link was drawn with `http://` before it (one copy, 2026-11-20).
  - Of the other withdrawn copies, most predate fixes for rows drawn beside a reply (M120); one was a table, which the screen draws as a box with each cell wrapped in place, so its text runs in another order.
- **Not verified:** whether the renderer draws only a link's text in a terminal that supports hyperlinks; no screen copy in the store was drawn so.
- **Current use:** a transcript copy is matched by its key and by the key with its links drawn this way (architecture.md § Reading a session, Hold).

## M131. The voice process's GPU use: recognisers never freed and silence decoded, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** the voice process on main at 5b0b15cd (build becb5158b3447af6); sherpa-onnx-node 2.0.1 with its CUDA 12 build and the fp32 Nemotron 0.6B 160 ms streaming model on an NVIDIA RTX 2000 Ada (16 GB), driver's per-process counters on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; Node.js v24.14.0.
- **Evidence:** the operator's `voice.log`, read only, `nvidia-smi` and the Windows counters `GPU Process Memory(*)\Dedicated Usage` and `GPU Engine(*)\Utilization Percentage`.
  - At 22:56 the GPU was at 90 to 97% use with 15.6 of 16 GB in use; the voice process (pid 47152) held 11.1 GB of dedicated GPU memory and its 3D engine ran at 93%, with the mic on since 22:54:27; Ollama's `llama-server` held 2.7 GB. Since its start at 22:26:33 the process had logged 7 `recogniser loaded ahead of the mic` lines, one per set of terms as the target moved between repositories, taking 4.4 to 15.0 s, the slowest last, as GPU memory ran out.
  - Once that process was replaced (22:57:52), the GPU fell to 9% and 3.8 GB was freed. The new process logged 4 loads in its first 2 minutes as the operator moved between sessions of different repositories, and held 11.1 GB by 23:21 with the mic off and no load since 23:00. A reading of 4.1 GB at 23:07 came just after the scratch processes below had each taken 3.3 to 6.6 GB, and was probably Windows moving its memory out of the GPU rather than a release.
- **Method:** scratch Node processes, not committed, loaded the CUDA build and model from the operator's models folder with no hub, capture or playback, and read their own counters; audio came from WAV files: a recorded utterance of the operator's from claude-collab's `.wip/audio`, followed by 30 s of -65 dBFS noise, fed in 100 ms chunks at real time, and the model's `test_wavs/1.wav`. Nothing played aloud.
- **Result:**
  - Each recogniser holds 3.3 GB of GPU memory. `OnlineRecognizer` has no destroy or free call, and `addon.createOnlineStream` takes only the recogniser (`Expect only 1 argument`), so hotwords cannot be set per stream or changed in place. The native recogniser is freed only when V8 collects its handle: dropping the reference left the memory in use; `gc()` freed it (3355 MiB → 87 MiB), but only on a later turn of the event loop, not before a timer of 0 ms, and in time for one of 20 ms. Without a collection, dropped recognisers stayed until V8 happened to collect, which the small JS objects rarely prompt: two loaded and dropped left 3.4 of 6.6 GB in use. `v8.setFlagsFromString('--expose-gc')` with `vm.runInNewContext('gc')` gives `gc` to a process started without the flag.
  - The voice check's speaker model ran on the CPU (`numThreads: 1`, no provider), once per piece; only the startup GPU check, a child process that exits, ran it on the GPU.
  - Every captured chunk was decoded, speech or silence. Unpaced, 20 s of digital silence took 5.0 s of decoding (124 decodes of 160 ms, 40 ms each) and 16.7 s of speech 4.1 s: about a quarter of real time either way. Paced at real time, room noise between words kept the 3D engine at 28.6% for that one stream on an otherwise idle GPU.
  - With decoding skipped below -55 dBFS after a 2 s hangover from the last loud audio or new words, and the half second before speech fed when it resumes, the same timeline gave the same pieces ("Um VS Code and the web view currently the composer VS Code is | the background"), and the 3D engine use over the noise fell from 28.6% to 0%, its decoding from 4600 ms to 129 ms. The operator's recordings sit at -60 to -75 dBFS between words and -40 to -20 dBFS in speech.
  - Freeing each recogniser before the next loads, with a collection, kept three successive loads at 3.35 GB.
- **Not verified:** whether the 93% was mostly decoding or WDDM paging GPU memory once it was full; the gate on capture without Windows' noise suppression, such as decibri's or a phone's, where room noise above -55 dBFS decodes as before; a newer sherpa-onnx with per-stream hotwords.
- **Current use:** the voice process frees a recogniser before loading one for other terms, and ahead of the mic waits for the memory to go first; recognition skips silence (architecture.md § Dictation).

## M132. Provider conversation title records, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Claude Code 2.1.330 and Codex CLI 0.171.0 on Windows.
- **Method:** inspected the repository's recorded Claude transcript fixtures (`packages/claude/test/fixtures/fork-plain-copy.jsonl` and `packages/claude/test/debrief.test.ts`), and generated Codex's installed app-server v2 schema with `codex app-server generate-json-schema` in a scratch directory. Compared the schema's `Thread`, `ThreadNameUpdatedNotification` and `thread/name/set` definitions with the hub adapters.
- **Result:** Claude transcript `custom-title` entries have `customTitle`; recorded `ai-title` entries have `aiTitle`. An existing Switchboard session launched with `-n` has a `custom-title` equal to Switchboard's launch name, which must be ignored when adopting provider titles. Codex's `thread/name/updated` notification carries `threadId` and nullable `threadName`; a `Thread` returned by `thread/read` or `thread/resume` carries nullable `name`. `thread/name/set` takes a name that would otherwise be set by Switchboard itself.
- **Not verified:** a live promptless Claude session without `-n` producing `ai-title`; which Codex client sets a thread name automatically rather than manually. The app-server notification does not say which did it.
- **Current use:** interactive work items start without Switchboard setting a native title; the hub follows provider titles and keeps a short initial label until one appears (architecture.md § Hosted conversations and § Standalone sessions).

## M133. Native and page swipe previews in an iOS Home Screen app, 2026-11-22

- **Environment:** the operator's iPhone Home Screen installation of the Switchboard page; iOS version and browser version were not recorded.
- **Observation:** a slow right swipe beginning at the screen's left edge, while a work item was open, showed three layers in the operator's screenshot: the prior work-item list coming in from the left, another copy of the list under the conversation, and the conversation moving right. A quick swipe hid the overlap. The page had created a browser history entry for the conversation and ran its own touch-driven back animation on the same edge swipe. The W3C Web App Manifest [navigation-gesture report](https://github.com/w3c/manifest/issues/1041) documents the same native and JavaScript gesture overlap in installed iOS web apps.
- **Inference:** the left copy was iOS's native history preview, and the middle copy was the page's own list. The screenshot establishes the overlapping layers, but no WebKit trace was captured to identify either compositor layer directly.
- **Current use:** when browser history has more than one entry, the page leaves an iOS left-edge swipe to browser history, which returns through the same `popstate` path as Back; a swipe begun farther in still uses the page's animation (architecture.md § Web page).
- **Not verified:** the fix on the iPhone, the width of iOS's native edge zone, a Safari or Chrome tab, a direct link with no earlier history entry, or whether the native preview's list is up to date.

## M134. Done list windowing, 2026-11-22

- **Environment:** Windows, headless Chrome 158.0.0.0, 1280 × 720 viewport, a scratch browser profile and a fake loopback hub on port 18779. The operator's hub and Switchboard home were not used.
- **Method:** saved the web assets at `2c0fc675` before the change, then served those and the final changed assets from the fake hub with explicit versioned script URLs, no HTTP caching and service worker registration disabled. The final benchmark included selection and resize anchoring (`windowRows.tsx` SHA-256 `06887ae2c70b001bccc57489f5c5ff83617741effafeab1370e9120834c07eff`). The fake stream supplied 1,000 Done rows, 900 in Today and 100 in Yesterday, both bands open, with title, done time, `dev:address-pr-review`, ticket pill and completion detail. Render-to-paint is the time from emitting the list to the second animation frame: seven samples, the initial hello and six draws from an empty list. The scroll run advanced from top to bottom over 180 animation frames and recorded 179 frame intervals. No CPU throttling was applied.
- **Measurements:** median render-to-paint 172.1 ms before, 33.3 ms after (81% lower). At the end of the scroll, 1,000 row buttons were mounted before and 18 after; both lists retained a 72,110 px scroll range. Scroll frame intervals had median 16.7 ms and p95 16.8 ms in both runs, maximum 16.9 ms before and 16.8 ms after, with zero intervals over 25 ms. Scrolling was already at the browser's frame cadence before the change; this run establishes reduced rendering work without a measured scroll regression.
- **Behavior checks:** a focused button survived scrolling out of view and a fresh list payload; selecting rows 990 and 1 mounted and scrolled them below the pinned heading; a closed band kept the selected row, reopening restored its count, Enter and Space toggled it while keeping focus, and searching for 995 showed that row. At 200 px list width, rows retained their existing layout and measured heights of 71, 72 and 92 px. After scrolling through wrapped rows and selecting row 500, changing the list from 200 to 320 and back to 200 px kept that row at y=300 px while adjusting the scroll offset. Independent headless checks with uniform 50 px rows and mixed 50 and 71 px rows kept distant selections and sequential selection steps in view, preserved manual scrolls after selection and cached heights while hidden, and kept visible content stable while scrolling through unmeasured rows. Discarding measured heights without anchoring moves visible content; holding the selected or visible row while spacer heights change preserves its position, and an intentional selection jump replaces the previous anchor.
- **Evidence:** `packages/web/test/windowRows.test.tsx` covers window bounds, pixel spacer heights, focus identity, offscreen selection with real scroll movement in the mock, selection anchoring, wrapping, resize and measurement anchoring, manual scrolling after selection, hiding the pane, closed bands and groups, filtering, unaffected groups and cleanup. The benchmark scripts, raw measurements and screenshots were session scratch artifacts and are not retained in the repository.
- **Limits:** these are one machine's synthetic list timings, including frame scheduling, not a CPU-only render benchmark or physical display smoothness measurement. The fake hub exercises the built page with injected stream and fetch peers, not production transport or a live agent. Safari, iOS and the parallel keyboard-navigation branch were not exercised by this run.

## M135. The web page's links in the Switchboard tab group, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Playwright's Chromium 155.0.8059.12 (`chromium-1247`), headless (`--headless=new`), scratch profiles, the extension loaded with `--load-extension` and `--disable-features=DisableLoadExtensionCommandLineSwitch`; Windows 11.
- **Method:** a scratch hub in its own home (`--browser-port=0`, its own page port, `sessions.installHooks` off, no sources), with one work item whose ticket was a GitHub issue and whose links were two GitHub pull requests and a page on another site, written into its store. A test HTTPS server on `127.0.0.1:8443` stood in for `tailscale serve`: it passed `pc.tail1234.ts.net` to the hub with `X-Forwarded-Proto: https` and an allowed `Tailscale-User-Login`, and answered every other host itself with a stub page, so no request left the computer; `--host-resolver-rules` mapped the names to it and certificate errors were ignored. The browser paired through `hub.js web pair`, downloaded `/page/switchboard-extension.zip` from the paired page, and was started again with that copy loaded; the script then clicked the row's pills on the page and read every tab group and tab from the service worker after each click. A second profile loaded `extensions/chrome/dist` and opened the scratch hub's loopback page from `hub.js web`, with `web.chromePane` off.
- **Result:**
  - The page on `https://pc.tail1234.ts.net` had `chrome.runtime.sendMessage` for the extension's ID, which the downloaded copy's `externally_connectable` granted for that origin, and the extension answered its `links` probe. A page on another HTTPS origin had no `chrome.runtime` at all.
  - The ticket's pill made a group titled Switchboard, coloured cyan, in the page's window, with the issue in a new active tab; the first pull request joined it as a second tab. Its pill again only brought that tab forward: no tab was added or loaded. The second pull request loaded in the first pull request's tab, and the other site's link joined as a third tab. The page's tab stayed outside the group.
  - On the loopback page with the copy from `dist`, the two pull requests' pills made the group and then reused its one pull request tab.
- **Not verified:** branded Chrome and Edge, which ignore `--load-extension` from Chrome 137; a real `tailscale serve`; the installed app's window, whose links would go to the last focused normal window; the late fallback tab after an extension that does not answer, which the unit tests cover with a fake; a web link in the conversation and a History link, which take the same path in the page's code.
- **Current use:** the web page's links in the Switchboard tab group on this computer and on another device (PRD R123; architecture.md § Browser extension).

## M136. Codex's model list in the app-server, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Codex CLI 0.171.0 on Windows.
- **Method:** generated the installed app-server's TypeScript bindings with `codex app-server generate-ts --out <scratch>` and read `ClientRequest`, `v2/ModelListParams`, `v2/ModelListResponse` and `v2/Model`. No live request was sent.
- **Result:** `model/list` takes optional `modelProvider`, `cursor`, `limit` and `includeHidden`, and answers `{data: Model[], nextCursor: string | null}`. A `Model` carries `id`, `model`, `displayName`, `description`, `hidden` (models left out of the default picker), `isDefault`, and its reasoning efforts and service tiers.
- **Not verified:** a live answer's contents for the operator's account, and which of `id` and `model` `thread/start` takes when they differ; the hub sends `model`.
- **Current use:** the New session form's Model list for Codex, asked once per hub start, without hidden models, falling back to `sessions.codex.model` alone when the request fails (PRD R122).

## M137. Chrome and the web page offline: a 502 page load, hung requests and a resent POST, 2026-11-22

- **Versions:** Google Chrome 158.0.8127.36, headless (`--headless=new`), on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; the web page on the offline-p1 branch and at 2c0fc675 before it; Node.js v24.14.0.
- **Method:** `packages/web/check-offline.mjs` against its own fake hub on a loopback port, which answers 502 with an empty body as `tailscale serve` does for a stopped hub (the plan's reproduction of that answer, not re-run against `tailscale serve` here), never answers, or destroys a connection after reading a request; the page at iPhone size through the DevTools protocol.
- **Result:**
  - A page load the network answers 502 shows Chrome's own "HTTP ERROR 502" page when the service worker passes the answer through (2c0fc675), and the page when the worker answers from Cache Storage instead; the page then runs from its kept script and stylesheets.
  - Requests a server never answers each keep one of Chrome's connections to the host open; after a hanging hub, the page's next requests to the same host stalled until the fake hub closed those sockets, so a page whose requests have no time limit can stop itself with its own hung requests.
  - When the server read a `POST` and destroyed the connection without answering, Chrome sent the same `POST` again by itself, with the same body, before the page's script saw an error: at 2c0fc675, whose page never retries a send, the fake hub got the send twice with one `opId`.
  - An `EventSource` whose connection is refused reconnects by itself and keeps the page; through a 502 it does not (the plan's finding), which is why the page owns its reconnect.
- **Not verified:** Safari and iOS, whose service worker and connection limits may differ; a real `tailscale serve` and cellular network.
- **Current use:** the app shell's 3 s navigation race that treats 502, 503 and 504 as no answer, the time limit on every page request, and the `opId` a send keeps across tries (architecture.md § Web page, App shell, Requests in time).

## M138. GitHub iframe headers and extension-hosted authentication, 2026-11-23

The single-frame experiment described below is superseded by R125's per-link tabs. R113's native pane is retired; its earlier measurements remain historical evidence.

- **Versions and setup:** Windows, Chrome for Testing 155.0.8059.12, the `experiment/iframe-browser` worktree, its built web page, hub PageAccess and page server under a scratch home, and its unpacked extension in isolated browser profiles. The live server uses recorded work item shapes and starts no sessions. To avoid the running hub's fixed browser endpoint, the test copy substitutes port 47915 for 47615 in the generated background and host bundles; source and installed builds retain 47615. Scratch scripts, logs and screenshots are under `.wip/iframe-browser/` in that worktree.
- **Framing:** a tab-specific declarativeNetRequest session rule removed X-Frame-Options and Content-Security-Policy only for exact HTTPS github.com subframe documents. Chrome accepted `topDomains` and returned it in getSessionRules. A public GitHub issue rendered in the actual Switchboard Browser area. Ordinary GitHub top-level responses still carried X-Frame-Options deny and CSP with frame-ancestors. Navigating the Switchboard tab to GitHub removed its rule, from one to zero. Chrome documents topDomains as Chrome 145+ in its [declarativeNetRequest API](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/extensions/reference/api/declarativeNetRequest).
- **Cookie probe:** synthetic, non-authentication cookies on github.com, one SameSite=Lax and one SameSite=Strict, were used to observe only whether each cookie was sent. With an extension page as the outermost page and a direct GitHub iframe, both were sent on the document and an in-frame fetch. With an ordinary HTTP page containing an extension iframe containing GitHub, both were sent for the first GitHub document but neither for the later GitHub fetch. A nested extension alone therefore does not preserve the session for application requests. This matches Chromium's [extension cookie exemptions](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/extensions/develop/concepts/storage-and-cookies).
- **Authenticated result:** after the operator signed into GitHub outside in the same isolated profile, the extension-hosted view reported a signed-in body and no Sign in link in its sibling GitHub frame, as the outside tab did. Reloading the entire host still produced a signed-in GitHub frame. Setting the sibling's src from the extension document loaded the private repository with status 200; an in-frame authenticated fetch of its issues also returned 200. A later check restarted the isolated browser with the same profile: GitHub remained signed in, selecting the private-repository work item loaded it with status 200, clicking its Issues link inside the frame succeeded, and a later authenticated fetch returned 200. Status and menu changes hid the sibling, returning to Browser preserved its navigated document and sign-in, the wrapper mirrored the page title, and the Switchboard child reported microphone and clipboard-write delegation enabled. A DevTools-driven frame.goto to the private URL returned a signed-out 404 in a separate check, so that command is not equivalent to extension-initiated navigation. No login cookie was copied, rewritten or printed for these probes.
- **Boundaries:** the local extension asks the fixed browser endpoint to vouch for the actual page origins before checking the page's sign-in. Remote copies use their exact configured HTTPS origin. The host accepts placement only from its Switchboard child, confines it to the viewport and exact GitHub URLs, and ignores GitHub messages. Header removal includes the entire CSP, not only frame-ancestors. The Switchboard document allows an ancestor of the one extension only while the experiment is enabled; its normal framing refusal remains otherwise.
- **Not established:** a live paired Tailscale device, branded Edge, Firefox, other websites, SSO or OAuth redirects, passkeys, long idle periods, actual microphone/clipboard prompts, or disabling the setting in a live hosted view. GitHub's auxiliary signed-out endpoints produced some 404 errors; rendering alone was not counted as authentication. No form was submitted and no source data was changed. The UI and route tests use fakes; these live observations are separate from those tests.
- **Current use:** the disabled-by-default `web.iframeBrowser` proof of concept (PRD R125). The replacement uses extension-hosted frames only; the ordinary page now launches that host.

- **Replacement acceptance, 2026-11-23:** the per-link pool loaded a public issue and the private repository in separate sibling frames. Clicking the private repository's Issues link, switching to another link and Status and back, and restarting the isolated page listener retained the same navigated document and its JavaScript marker; an in-frame fetch of private issues returned 200. Two rapid launches reused one host without resetting that document. Launching a work item whose earlier fragment was stale selected the requested work item, and closing then reopening a link created a fresh frame. A view-options menu hid every website frame. After consuming a launch fragment, reloading only the trusted Switchboard child stayed on the work item selected since that launch. The ordinary page had no header rule. These were read-only GitHub actions in the existing isolated signed-in profile; the listener restart reuses the test store in one process, not a production hub process restart. The final UI was inspected with its scrolling link strip. Branded Edge and an actual paired remote device remain unverified.

- **Host focus acceptance:** with the private GitHub frame focused, the trusted Switchboard child reported document focus false while the host had focus and the fixture received page focus true. Minimizing the host and restoring it into GitHub produced page-focus false then true, with no repeated unchanged reports. A fake-clock unit check separately covers missed outer focus events, hidden tabs and app-load resend. This checks routing signals, not microphone hardware or spoken output.

## M139. Arbitrary web links in extension-hosted tabs, 2026-11-23

- **Setup:** Windows, Chrome for Testing 155.0.8059.12, the iframe-browser worktree's built page and extension, isolated persistent profiles, real PageAccess and page listener with recorded work items, no sessions or source writes. Scratch harness and screenshot: `.wip/all-links/`. The test extension alone substitutes browser port 47915, as M138 does. A self-signed local HTTPS fixture uses a test-only certificate bypass; shipped browser launch settings are unchanged.
- **Links and documents:** selecting a work item exposed sixteen tabs, including HTTP, HTTPS, Zoho and Acme forum links. Frames loaded lazily. HTTP at 127.0.0.1 and HTTPS at localhost loaded despite X-Frame-Options DENY and CSP frame-ancestors none; their inline script ran despite the fixture's script-src none. Switching tabs retained a form value and its document; Reload recreated the document. A redirect from the HTTP host to the HTTPS host rendered. Closing the browser and reopening the same profile preserved the Switchboard sign-in.
- **Cookies:** synthetic non-authentication SameSite=Lax and SameSite=Strict cookies were sent on in-frame fetches at both hosts. Session cookies without expiry ended with the browser session, as expected. Repeating with Max-Age=86400 preserved both cookies across a full browser restart; only boolean presence was inspected. No real authentication cookie was copied or printed. M138 remains the authenticated GitHub evidence; this run did not establish authenticated Zoho or Forum access.
- **Rule boundaries:** Chrome accepted exact-host-and-port URL regexes and initiatorDomains (extension ID, that same host or its subdomains), restricted by tab ID, extension topDomains and sub_frame. A nested cross-site iframe from 127.0.0.1 to localhost was refused even though localhost had a sibling tab. The same refusing fixture in an ordinary browser tab's iframe was refused. The trusted Switchboard child still blocked an injected inline script; its response retained its normal CSP and X-Frame-Options. DevTools reported the original website response headers even where the browser visibly applied the removal, so rendering and script execution established the effective website behavior. A higher-priority allow rule protects the exact hub origin and its loopback alias at the same port; Chrome documents allow priority in its [declarativeNetRequest API](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/extensions/reference/api/declarativeNetRequest). Other listed localhost ports remained embeddable. Host/controller and launcher unit checks refuse hub-origin and browser-endpoint links.
- **Rule IDs:** live Chrome exposed tab IDs large enough that doubling them exceeded the signed 32-bit DNR rule-ID range. Rules now allocate independent IDs and retain them in leases; a unit check covers tab ID 2147483640, replacement, missing rules and cleanup.
- **Pills:** Ctrl+click on another work item's ticket invoked the fixture's row-open command without changing selection. A normal link pill selected its work item, focused the requested embedded tab, disposed the earlier work item's frames and created no outside tab. This exposed and fixed a race with site-rule installation; unit checks cover delayed readiness, late selection events and refused selections. The existing hub command owns the actual operating-system default-browser launch; the live fixture records that request instead of launching a user browser.
- **Public sites and limits:** read-only Zoho Desk redirected to its public product page and rendered; Acme's public forum index rendered. No ticket login or source write was attempted. Cross-site navigation initiated by a website retains its framing restrictions and may need Open outside. This does not establish branded Edge, a live paired device, arbitrary SSO flows, passkeys or every site's authentication behavior. The setting remains opt-in.

- **Exact host follow-up:** DNR domain lists include subdomains, so a listed broad domain could otherwise widen removal to unrelated descendants. Request matching now uses an escaped exact host and port regex. An expanded nineteen-link fixture loaded an IPv6 loopback page, a parent test domain and its child domain; the child's attempt to nest an unlisted sibling domain was refused. Chrome accepted bracketed IPv6 and punycode hosts in initiatorDomains. Unit checks cover com, github.io, exact ports, IPv6 and punycode, and rule readiness tracks ports as well as hostnames. Test-only hostname resolution mapped those domains to the fixture listener.

## M140. What Claude Code queues itself at a usage limit, 2026-11-23

- **Versions:** Claude Code CLI 2.1.328 to 2.1.330 on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; the operator's terminal transcripts under `~/.claude/projects`, read only, and strings in the 2.1.330 binary.
- **Inspection:** every `queue-operation` `enqueue` line whose content starts with `/` or names the usage limit, the lines around them, the `origin` kinds of user lines, and the binary's strings for `rate-limit-options`. No limit was forced.
- **Result:**
  - When a turn reaches a 5-hour usage limit and Claude Code arms its automatic continue, it queues `/rate-limit-options` right after the turn's last reply (28 ms after it, in the one case seen), followed by a `dequeue` and a `system` `informational` line `Usage limit reached · set to continue automatically at 11:20am`. The queue line records only `type`, `operation`, `timestamp`, `sessionId` and `content`, as M118 found for every queue line. No user line, attachment or command output records the command being run. In the binary `rate-limit-options` is a `local_jsx` dialog command (the menu of upgrade, extra usage, auto-resume and cancel choices).
  - At the reset Claude Code queues `Your claude.ai usage limit has reset. Continue the task you were working on when the limit was reached; do not repeat work that is already complete.`, then writes `Usage limit reset · Continuing automatically` and receives the text as a user line with `isMeta: true`, `origin` `{"kind":"auto-continuation"}`, `promptSource` `system` and `turnOrigin` `auto_continuation` (13 such lines seen).
  - The 2.1.330 binary holds a second continue text for usage that comes back before the reset, `Your claude.ai usage is available again before the usage-limit reset. Continue the task you were working on when the limit was reached; do not repeat work that is already complete.`, which it queues and recognises as its own as it does the first; no transcript held it. The same check also accepts a continue text read from a setting, which was not traced.
  - Its other usage-limit notes, such as the `[Usage limit reached; a short grace allowance remains, …]` line with `usageLimitNote` `wrap_up`, are user lines with `isMeta: true` and are not queued.
  - Before Switchboard left them out, the two queue lines, having no origin, showed in the conversation as provisional messages from the operator, which stayed until a later prompt line withdrew them.
- **Not verified:** whether a 7-day limit, an extra-usage or a throttle limit queues the same command, and whether Claude Code queues `/rate-limit-options` when automatic continue is off.
- **Current use:** a queue line holding exactly the command or one of the two continue texts is left out of the conversation, so an operator message with other words beside them still shows (architecture.md § Reading a session).

## M141. Text near a turning spinner in Chrome, 2026-11-23

- **Versions:** Google Chrome 158.0.8127.36, headless (`--headless=new`), with and without `--disable-gpu`, on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; the web page's built stylesheets at 7e6adad6 and on the spinner-isolate branch.
- **Method:** a scratch page with the page's built `theme.css`, `codicon.css` and `page.css` and copies of the page's spinner markup (the Thinking and background-agents lines, a list row's spinning mark, Opening…, the session picker's state icon and the mic's starting ring), each below a line of text, at device pixel ratios 1, 1.25 and 1.5 through the DevTools protocol: the layer tree (`LayerTree`), a 1.5 s trace of `Paint` events, and 40 screenshots of each spinner's area compared outside the spinners' boxes, and against the same page with every spinner stopped.
- **Result:**
  - Each spinner's `transform` animation, `steps(30)` or linear, runs on the compositor: no `Paint` event in 1.5 s and no pixel outside a spinner's box changed between frames, at every ratio, so a spinner's step repaints no neighbouring text.
  - Chrome takes a layer whose transform animates to overlap everything painted after it up to its nearest clip, and moves that content onto layers of its own: the background-agents line after a Thinking spinner, a whole list row after an earlier spinner, and the composer after the session picker's spinner. Text on those layers lost its subpixel (LCD) antialiasing: up to 3135 pixels differed from the page with the spinners stopped, at every ratio.
  - Turning only the spinner's `::before` inside a box with `contain: paint` (an overflow clip) left one layer per spinner and none for the content after it; the text then matched the stopped page pixel for pixel. `will-change: transform` and `contain: layout paint` on the turning element itself left those layers in place. Layout containment takes an inline-block's baseline away, so it is not used.
- **Not verified:** the operator's glitch itself, a line above a spinner drawn split and jumping by about a pixel, was not reproduced: headless Chrome drew no change between frames before or after the fix. The hypothesis is that the text moves between its own layer and the page's as rows, spinners and the layers they cause come and go, or that a raster tile at a fractional ratio splits it. A GPU-backed, windowed Chrome, Edge, Safari and iOS were not tried.
- **Current use:** the page's shared spinner rule in `packages/web/src/page.css`.

## M142. Cached page CSP blocks the extension host after enabling embedding, 2026-11-23

- **Setup:** Windows, Microsoft Edge 158.0.4370.33, the iframe-host-cache worktree, an isolated persistent Edge profile with the unpacked extension, real PageAccess and a test-owned page listener serving the built page. The fixture starts with embedding disabled, then changes it in the same process; no source writes or agent sessions run. The test copy alone substitutes browser endpoint 47915 to avoid the installed hub. Scratch evidence is under `.wip/iframe-host-cache/` in that worktree.
- **Before:** load Switchboard with embedding disabled, enable it, reload and choose Open with sign-in support. The status endpoint says enabled, but a normal fetch of the page still reports its cached CSP with frame-ancestors none. The extension host's app iframe stays on a blocked about:blank document and Edge paints its refusal screen. The server's HTML ETag hashes only the body, which has not changed, so it sends 304 without replacing the cached CSP. A hub regression reproduces that 304 when a 200 with the new policy is required.
- **After:** the HTML ETag hashes the body and effective CSP. In the same Edge profile, enabling embedding changes the validator and replaces the cached refusal with the extension-only ancestor policy. Reloading the previously blocked host shows the signed-in Switchboard page, and opening a fresh host through Open with sign-in support also shows it. Unchanged policy still revalidates with 304; the regression checks that turning embedding off replaces a cached permission with frame-ancestors none and retains X-Frame-Options DENY. Website header exceptions and sign-in cookies are unchanged.
- **Limits:** this proves the local Edge cache and host-loading path. It does not add authentication evidence for external sites or a paired remote device. Existing blocked tabs need a reload after the hub update.

## M143. Edge resets iframe scroll when display is removed, 2026-11-23

- **Setup:** Windows, Microsoft Edge 158.0.4370.33, the iframe-host-cache worktree, a test-owned hub/page listener and HTTP fixture, isolated profiles and the unpacked extension. The test copy alone uses browser endpoint 47915. Two pages refuse normal framing and expose a document marker, a form, tall content and request counts. The fixture sends the hub's tabs event when a tab choice is saved. Scratch evidence is under `.wip/iframe-tabs/`.
- **Before:** switching Ticket → Second → Ticket within one work item kept the first document's JavaScript marker and form text, with one request per page, but reset its scroll position from 600 to 0. The iframe remained attached; setting its hidden attribute removed its rendered viewport. This reproduces the reported reload effect without a new document request.
- **After:** inactive frames retain their dimensions with visibility hidden and inert, and aria-hidden keeps them out of the accessible content. The same switch retained the marker, form text and scroll position 600, with one request per page. Status and a view-options menu also hid frames without losing that position; the menu made every website frame invisible and inert. A public GitHub issue retained its document marker and scroll position 900 when another link tab showed and it returned. GitHub uses smooth scrolling, so the probe waited for its starting position before switching. No external form was submitted or source data changed.
- **Hosted layout:** the extra Reload/Open outside controls and hint are removed only inside the extension host. At wide and narrow desktop sizes, the slot rectangle matched the content pane and its padding was 0; the ordinary page retained its host-launch and outside controls. This verifies Edge on the local computer, not a paired device or every external site's own refresh behavior.
- **Selected tab edge:** the scroll wrapper clipped the selected tab's overlap of the strip border. Giving the wrapper the same one-pixel overlap and omitting the reserved scrollbar row let the selected tab meet the content edge. Light and dark Edge checks showed the tab extending past the strip divider with its bottom border matching its surface; Shift+wheel scrolled an overflowing strip by 60 px, and keyboard focus revealed its clipped tab. Screenshots: `tabs-light.png` and `tabs-dark.png`.

## M144. Extension URL in Chrome's app window and sandbox attachment warning, 2026-11-23

The offline startup limitation below is superseded by M145's verified-origin shell fallback.

- **Setup:** Windows, Google Chrome 158.0.8127.36, the iframe-host-cache worktree, its current built extension, and the test-owned listener from M143. An isolated headed Chrome profile loaded a test copy through `Extensions.loadUnpacked` with `--enable-unsafe-extension-debugging`; only that copy used browser endpoint 47915. Scratch plan and browser logs are under `.wip/app-launch/`. The installed extension was also opened read-only in the operator's existing Chrome profile; no browser settings, source data or authentication were changed.
- **Launch and title bar:** starting Chrome with the same `--user-data-dir`, `--profile-directory=Default` and `--app=chrome-extension://hlbibnhgkgnjkbfledhgjblacloneije/iframeHost.html?origin=http%3A%2F%2F127.0.0.1%3A64926` opened an app window. `chrome.windows.getAll()` reported type `app`; the outer document retained its extension origin and loaded the Switchboard child and website sibling. The native window showed Switchboard's icon and title, minimize, maximize and close, without a visible address bar or browser tab strip. Dark and light content rendered. Launching the installed host for origin 47616 with `--profile-directory="Profile 1"` and no debugging flags also opened that title bar, the current work item and its signed-in private GitHub page. This proves a launch into an already-running browser, not full browser restart, taskbar pinning, grouping, shortcut icons or installed-PWA registration. Each direct launch may create another window; the extension's normal host reuse is a different path.
- **Web code and offline boundary:** inside the app window, the Switchboard child reported its JavaScript started, `display-mode: standalone`, an active service-worker controller, one registration and a `switchboard-shell` cache. With the isolated browser offline, reloading the outer extension document left the Switchboard iframe hidden on about:blank and showed the host's sign-in/setting note: its live origin and enabled checks failed before loading the cached child. Reloading only the already-open Switchboard child while offline did load its cached JavaScript, set its started flag and show Connecting to Switchboard, with an active service-worker controller. Restoring connectivity and reloading recovered. This limitation also applies to the normal extension-hosted tab; app-window launch does not remove the service worker. The cache holds the app shell, not offline work items or conversations. A cached offline shell on fresh host startup needs a separate design that preserves origin validation and refuses website header exceptions while unverified.
- **Sandbox warning:** Chrome warned when the website frame, carrying `allow-scripts` and `allow-same-origin`, was appended before its URL was assigned, while its initial about:blank shared the extension's origin. Setting the validated HTTP(S) URL before attachment removed that warning in the same browser and still loaded the website. Every sandbox token remains, including the absence of top-navigation permission. The unit regression checks the URL and sandbox at attachment; the live rebuilt host had zero sandbox warnings. The optional missing local `remote.json` fetch still produced its separate resource diagnostic.

## M145. Windows app shortcut and verified-origin offline shell, 2026-11-23

- **Setup:** Google Chrome 158.0.8127.36 on Windows, the iframe-host-cache worktree, its built page and browser extension in an isolated headed profile, test-owned page/browser listeners on ports 64926/47915, no agent sessions or source writes. Only the test extension substitutes browser endpoint 47915. Scratch plan, checks and live results are under `.wip/app-install/`.
- **Offline reload:** a live app-window host verified its origin and saved that exact origin in extension-local storage. With the isolated context offline, reloading the outer extension document loaded the inner cached shell: its started flag and service-worker controller were true, the host note was hidden and there were zero website frames. Restoring connectivity brought the work item's website frame back without another outer reload. This proves shell execution and reconnect, not offline work items or conversations; the cache keeps no API data.
- **Refusal:** disabling embedding through the fixture made the open host hide its child, show the sign-in/setting note and remove the saved origin. A subsequent offline outer reload kept the child hidden on about:blank with the connect-once note. Sending the disable message from the trusted app child also cleared the saved key before navigating outside; the next offline host launch was refused. Unit checks also cover unknown origins, malformed and unauthenticated responses, refused local origin lists, storage failures, concurrent probes and saves, and blocking website messages while unverified. Repeated authority checks do not renew the app's frame-placement heartbeat. Website header leases retain their live checks.
- **Superseded shortcut experiment:** Windows tests created a real `.lnk` in a test-owned temporary directory through WScript.Shell, read back its target, arguments and icon, ran the local CLI command twice to update its origin, and verified that an unrelated existing link was refused and preserved. The generated arguments named the browser data directory, profile and extension host with the encoded origin, with no pairing token. The icon wrapped the shipped 128 px PNG in an ICO under the Switchboard home. Browser selection and failures used injected metadata/files/processes; the tests never wrote the real Start menu or desktop. M146 replaces this installation approach.
- **Shortcut launch:** opening a generated `.lnk` in the worktree's scratch folder targeted the isolated Chrome profile and created another window reported as `app`; it loaded the extension host, Switchboard child and website sibling. The test closed only that added window. This was a launch into an existing browser process.
- **Limits:** taskbar pinning/grouping, other authentication providers, Edge's app-window appearance and full browser restart are not established by these checks. M144 remains the native Chrome title-bar and installed-profile sign-in evidence.

## M146. Portable Windows shortcut download from the web page, 2026-11-23

- **Format:** a pure Node buffer generator uses Microsoft's [MS-SHLLINK format](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/windows_protocols/ms-shllink): a Unicode name and arguments, an EnvironmentVariableDataBlock with PreferEnvironmentPath, and no machine-specific ID list, LinkInfo or tracking data. The target is a fixed Chrome or Edge executable path under Program Files or Local AppData, expanded on the receiving computer. Only the profile directory and a fixed installation choice are accepted; the authenticated request supplies the hub origin. No installer, script, pairing token or server path is included.
- **Native check:** Windows WScript.Shell reads the generated `.lnk`, its extension-host URL, optional profile and client-expanded target correctly. A bare `chrome.exe` target instead resolved to a nonexistent file under the desktop and could not launch; the portable file therefore names the browser's environment-based installation path. Without an icon file Windows uses the browser icon; the extension's app window still has Switchboard's own icon and title. Tests write only a temporary folder.
- **Scope:** this replaces M145's local CLI installation experiment at the operator's request. The offline shell and live verification behavior from M145 remain. Local and paired pages both offer Download app shortcut; paired downloads bind their own HTTPS origin rather than the hub's loopback address. Browser/profile installation is not inferred from the hub computer.
- **Browser download:** Chrome 158 downloaded a direct `.lnk` with the suggested filename `Switchboard.download`, despite the attachment and download attribute naming `.lnk`. The web action therefore gives a zip containing only `Switchboard.lnk`; the user extracts and places that file. No script or installer is included.
- **Paired browser check:** an isolated Chrome 158 profile paired through real PageAccess to a test HTTPS proxy for `pc.shortcut.test`, standing in for Tailscale on test-owned loopback listeners. More Actions → Download app shortcut downloaded `switchboard-shortcut.zip` without failure; it contained only `Switchboard.lnk`. Windows read its target and encoded HTTPS origin correctly. Loading the file's extension-host URL in the same browser, with the extension copy downloaded from the paired page, loaded the trusted HTTPS child and its website sibling with the pairing cookie. Scratch evidence is under `.wip/web-shortcut/`. This verifies the paired route and document behavior, not a real Tailscale device or a new native launch of this particular downloaded file; M144 remains the app-window launch evidence.

## M147. Windows shortcut identity and icons, 2026-11-23

- **Correction to M146:** the operator confirmed that the desktop shortcut opened the app, but pinning its running Edge window produced an Edge pin that reopened a plain browser. The shortcut had no icon and the app icon was low resolution. M146's assumption that an icon-less link would use the browser icon was not established by its parsing check.
- **Source inspection:** Chromium's [Windows window properties](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/refs/heads/main/chrome/browser/ui/views/frame/browser_window_property_manager_win.cc) assign URL app windows a separate AppUserModelID but leave their relaunch details empty when there is no registered app. Its [shell identity](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/refs/heads/main/chrome/browser/shell_integration_win.cc) and [identity builder](https://chromium.googlesource.com/chromium/src/+/refs/heads/main/chrome/installer/util/shell_util.cc) combine the browser prefix, host/path app name and profile component, truncate each component in the middle to fit 64 characters and replace spaces. The default data directory's Default profile omits the profile component. The URL query does not distinguish identities, so two hubs in the same profile share a taskbar identity.
- **Native properties:** an isolated Edge 158 app window for the extension host had AppUserModelID `MSEdge.hlbibnhgkgeHost.html.edgeprofile.Default`, matching the algorithm for the test data directory, and empty RelaunchCommand, RelaunchIconResource and RelaunchDisplayNameResource properties. Windows WScript.Shell and Shell.Application read the revised generated link's target, explicit profile, icon path and AppUserModelID correctly. A relative icon filename beside a shortcut produced a blank icon when read through SHGetFileInfo from a different current folder; the download therefore uses the browser-reported absolute icon path.
- **Icons and download:** the host and shortcut share an ICO with 16, 24, 32, 48, 64, 128 and 256 px images derived from the shipped 512 px Switchboard icon. The Chrome/Edge extension saves it to its browser's Downloads/Switchboard folder and returns the actual completed download location. Firefox strips the Downloads permission and does not include this Chrome-only listener.
- **Live download and shell icon:** isolated Edge downloaded the icon and shortcut zip through the web dialog; SHGetFileInfo returned the Switchboard icon for the extracted link. Chrome-extension resource URLs failed in the Downloads API, so the extension first reads its own fixed packaged icon and downloads its bytes as a data URL. Browser automation requires a Windows-format download path; a forward-slash CDP path caused FILE_FAILED. The actual browser-reported icon basename can differ, so the generator accepts a local absolute `.ico` path rather than assuming a basename. Native checks for both Default and Profile 1 caught and now cover VT_LPWSTR padding to a four-byte boundary; without it, Windows ignored Default's identity. The dialog was checked in light and dark themes, and the real VS Code suite passed 110 tests.
- **Limits:** actual taskbar pin, close and relaunch remains unverified. Native capture returned black, and activating the isolated Edge window failed with `GetCursorPos failed: Access is denied. (0x80070005)`. A matching shortcut identity is the proposed fix; source inspection and COM parsing do not prove taskbar registration or pinning. No user desktop, taskbar or Start menu entry was changed during these checks. Scratch evidence is in `.wip/shortcut-polish/`.
- **Operator acceptance:** after the icon and taskbar identity fixes were installed, the operator confirmed that the revised shortcut was working and requested integration into main on 2026-11-23. This closes the acceptance gap above for the reported shortcut issues; a full browser restart was not separately reported.

## M148. A held session without Code Mode, 2026-11-23

- **Versions:** Claude Code CLI 2.1.330 (native build), Haiku 5.5, on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; Node.js 24.14.0, node-pty 1.1.0, @xterm/headless 6.0.0.
- **Experiment:** a scratch Switchboard home with the generated `hook.mjs` and `contract.mjs`, and the built terminal holder (`packages/holder/dist/main.js`) started detached on it. `CodeModeRunner` from `packages/claude/dist` started a session with `withoutCodeMode` through a wrapper of the holder client that added Switchboard's hook entries for the scratch home to the `--settings` JSON and put `--setting-sources project,local` first, so the operator's user settings, and with them the hooks of the live Switchboard home, did not load. The command was `claude --session-id <uuid> --permission-mode manual -n <name> --model haiku --append-system-prompt <contract with a code word, then the panel, link and conversation sections> --settings {"viewMode":"focus","tui":"default"} <prompt asking for the code word>`, in a pseudo-terminal of 120×40 in a trusted git worktree, with `SWITCHBOARD_LAUNCH` set and no `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_CODE_MODE`. The session was then killed in the holder and resumed with `CodeModeRunner.resumeHeld` (`--resume <uuid>` with the same flags and a new launch), and a question was typed into it.
- **Result:**
  - The start check passed 2.1 s after the spawn on the launch's first marker; the screen showed no Code Mode line of either kind, and the first turn answered with the code word.
  - Each hook marker carried the launch, as in Code Mode: the first turn's `Stop`, then the resume's `SessionStart` `resume` under the new launch. `CodeModeRunner.observe` read the session as idle after each turn.
  - The transcript was `<uuid>.jsonl` with no `repl-mode` line; `transcriptLaunch` read `codeMode: false`, `permissionMode: manual` and the model `claude-haiku-5-5`.
  - The resumed session answered the typed question with the code word, so the appended prompt and conversation carried over.
- **Pass criteria:** a held interactive `claude` without Code Mode starts under the holder, writes markers with its launch through Switchboard's hooks, runs its prompt as the first turn, ends in the holder and resumes there without Code Mode. **Passed.**
- **Not verified:** the operator's user settings, gateway and own hooks (left out by `--setting-sources`); a start prompt such as folder trust; Linux.
- **Current use:** held Claude sessions with `sessions.codeMode` off, and their start check, which waits for the launch's first marker rather than the Code Mode line (architecture.md § Code Mode sessions).

## M149. A held session's suggested prompt under NO_COLOR, 2026-11-24

- **Versions:** Claude Code CLI 2.1.331 (native build), Haiku 5.5, on Windows 11 Pro 10.0.26300; Node.js 24.14.0, node-pty 1.1.0, @xterm/headless 6.0.0.
- **Symptom:** a message sent to an idle held session waited on "the input box holds a draft" until a key was typed in its terminal (hub.log 2026-11-24, session 311b1ad4: guards of 149 s and 255 s); messages sent mid-turn went at once.
- **Experiment:** `.wip/ghost-probe/` ran `claude --setting-sources project,local --model haiku` in node-pty at 100×30 with `TERM=xterm-256color` and the hub's environment less roughly the variables `codeModeEnvironment` removes, replayed its output in @xterm/headless and read the input box's cells after each reply, once with `NO_COLOR=1` and once without. The environment of the operator's running hub, holder and held `claude.exe` was read from their process blocks, without changing them.
- **Result:**
  - With colour, Claude Code writes a suggested prompt such as "Goldfish name?" after "❯ " as `ESC[2m…ESC[22m` and moves the cursor back to its start; the dim cells read as an empty box.
  - With `NO_COLOR=1`, Claude Code styles nothing (its output has only a reset, `ESC[m`): the suggestion "Name a dog" has the same cells as typed text, so it reads as a draft. The cursor does not tell them apart either: typed text after Home leaves the cursor at the box's start, as a suggestion does.
  - The operator's hub, holder and held `claude.exe` all had `NO_COLOR=1`; the supervisor that starts the hub did not. A Claude session's Bash tool sets `NO_COLOR=1`, so a hub started from one, and every hub that takes over from it, passes it on.
